<!-- BEGIN: references/tear-downs/i-pass-handoff.md -->

# Tear-Down: I-PASS Handoff Bundle (Starmer et al., NEJM 2014)

**Originating team:** Amy J. Starmer, Theodore C. Sectish, Christopher P. Landrigan, and the I-PASS Study Group (Boston Children's Hospital; nine pediatric residency sites)
**Canonical citation:** Starmer AJ, Spector ND, Srivastava R, et al. "Changes in medical errors after implementation of a handoff program." *N Engl J Med.* 2014;371(19):1803-1812. doi:10.1056/NEJMsa1405556
**Where to study:** the NEJM paper (free at PubMed Central PMC4244954); the I-PASS Institute curriculum (ipassinstitute.com); the mnemonic card itself (single laminated index card distributed to residents)
**What the artifact actually is:** a five-element verbal mnemonic, paired with a written printed sign-out template, paired with a mandatory receiver read-back — three coupled artifacts that together constitute *the* handoff

## What it is

I-PASS is a structured handoff bundle in which the outgoing clinician delivers each patient's status across five fixed elements — **I**llness severity, **P**atient summary, **A**ction list, **S**ituation awareness and contingency planning, and **S**ynthesis by receiver — using a printed sign-out document as visual scaffold, and ending each patient with the receiver speaking back the synthesis aloud. The bundle was developed in 2010-2011 at Boston Children's, formalised across nine pediatric residency programs, and tested in a stepped-wedge prospective study (10,740 patient admissions). Implementation produced a 23% reduction in medical errors (from 24.5 to 18.8 per 100 admissions) and a 30% reduction in *preventable* adverse events (from 4.7 to 3.3 per 100 admissions) — one of the largest effect sizes in modern patient-safety literature, achieved without new technology, new staffing, or new equipment. The artifact is a mnemonic and a piece of paper and a sentence the receiver has to say.

## Why it's apex

I-PASS is the patient-safety field's clearest example of *the form is the intervention*. Most quality-improvement bundles add steps to clinical work and produce small effects. I-PASS *replaces* an existing artifact (the unstructured verbal sign-out) with a structured one, and the effect size approaches double-digit absolute risk reduction. The bundle teaches what apex looks like for high-stakes communication artifacts in four ways: (1) **the mnemonic is a forcing function**, not a checklist — the receiver cannot complete S without having heard I-P-A-S; (2) **read-back closes the loop** — the receiver's synthesis is mandatory and surfaces gaps before the sender leaves; (3) **the written and verbal artifacts are coupled** — neither alone is the handoff; (4) **the bundle is rule-shaped, not protocol-shaped** — five elements, no more, no per-specialty variants, no escape hatches. The discipline of saying "no" to additions is what kept the form portable across the nine sites in the original trial and across the >50 hospitals that have since implemented it.

## Decomposition

### 1. The mnemonic is ordered, and the order is the argument

I before P before A before S before S. The order encodes the receiver's cognitive priorities: *how sick* before *who they are* before *what to do* before *what might go wrong* before *do I have it*. A residency program tempted to "improve" the order — leading with the patient summary, say — destroys the cognitive grammar. Illness severity comes first because at minute four of a forty-patient sign-out the receiver's first question is always *which patients on this list might die tonight*. Putting that answer first re-orders the listener's attention before any other information enters. The order is not negotiable.

### 2. Illness severity is a three-state classification, not a free-text field

The "I" element forces the sender into one of three states: **stable**, **watcher**, or **unstable**. Three states, no fourth option, no "complicated stable." The constraint is the move — by ruling out free text, the form prevents the cognitive failure mode of "this patient is doing okay but..." which the receiver interprets as stable while the sender meant watcher. The three-state classification is the cognitive analogue of triage-tag colors: ordinal, mutually exclusive, exhaustive. The receiver hears "watcher" and the patient gets a chart-flag in the receiver's mental rounding list before any other detail registers.

### 3. The contingency element is structured as IF/THEN pairs

The "S" for situation awareness is not "things to watch out for." It is *contingency planning*: explicit IF/THEN pairs. *If* the patient's heart rate exceeds 130, *then* call the senior and get a lactate. *If* urine output drops below 30 mL/hour for two hours, *then* a 500 mL fluid bolus is appropriate, hold further fluids without re-evaluation. The discipline of writing in IF/THEN converts vague clinical worry into pre-authorised receiver action. The cross-cover resident at 3 a.m. is not asked to *think*; they are asked to *recognise the trigger and execute the THEN*. This is the same cognitive offloading principle as ACLS algorithms, applied to per-patient anticipation.

### 4. Synthesis-by-receiver is the closed-loop element

The final "S" is not a sender action. It is the receiver speaking back — in their own words — the synthesis of what they just heard. "So Mr. Garcia is a watcher post-CABG, BP holding on low-dose norepinephrine, the contingency is to call you for sustained MAP under 65, and the action items are the morning labs and the cardiology consult note." The sender listens for what is wrong in the read-back and corrects in the moment. Without the read-back the handoff is a monologue; with it, the handoff is a closed communication loop. The cognitive science (Salas, Wilson, Murphy, King — crew resource management literature) treats read-back as the only reliable detector of mis-encoding at the receiver. Verbal-only "you got that?" is not read-back; read-back requires synthesis in the receiver's own words.

### 5. The printed sign-out document is the visual scaffold

The verbal bundle is paired with a printed document the receiver holds. The document is structured to mirror the mnemonic: a per-patient block with I-rating in a colored badge, patient summary as a 3-line problem-representation, action list as numbered items with checkboxes, contingencies as IF/THEN rows. The visual scaffold serves two jobs: it keeps the sender on the mnemonic (no patient is finished until all five elements are filled), and it gives the receiver a referenceable artifact for the next twelve hours. The original I-PASS paper specified the column layout in detail; subsequent implementations have varied the visual treatment while preserving the data schema. The document and the verbal exchange are not redundant — they encode the same data in two modalities so the receiver has the *spoken context* plus the *written referent* when they re-encounter the patient at 3 a.m.

### 6. The bundle resists per-specialty variants

When I-PASS spread from pediatrics to adult medicine, surgery, ICU, ED, and obstetrics, each specialty pressured for variants — "we need a sixth element for vent settings," "we need to split A into A-now and A-overnight." The I-PASS Institute resisted: the five elements stay; specialty content goes *inside* P and A and S, not as new top-level elements. The resistance is the move. Mnemonic discipline lives or dies on portability; a six-element variant in surgery breaks the receiver's habit when they rotate through medicine. The five-element form is the schema; the content slots are infinitely flexible. The form's authors understood that mnemonics calcify when they hit cognitive load limits, and chose to keep the schema portable rather than locally optimal.

### 7. The NEJM trial design is the implementation evidence, not opinion

The 30% reduction in preventable adverse events did not come from expert consensus or before/after self-report. It came from a *stepped-wedge prospective trial*: nine sites implemented I-PASS in randomised sequence over 18 months, with blinded adjudication of every adverse event by two pediatricians using validated rating instruments (Adverse Event Detection System, Modified Naranjo Algorithm). The trial design is part of what makes I-PASS credible as a quality intervention rather than a wellness initiative. The bundle's authority is borrowed from the trial; without the trial, I-PASS would be one more handoff mnemonic in a field that has at least a dozen (SBAR, SIGN-OUT, ANTICIPate, HAND-IT, SAFETY). The trial *settled* the field.

### 8. The contrast with SBAR is informative

SBAR (**S**ituation, **B**ackground, **A**ssessment, **R**ecommendation), developed by the U.S. Navy nuclear submarine program and ported to healthcare by Kaiser Permanente in the early 2000s, is the field's other major mnemonic. SBAR and I-PASS are not competitors; they answer different questions. SBAR is a *single-issue communication tool* — the nurse calling the resident about one deteriorating patient ("S: Mr. Lee is in respiratory distress; B: post-op day 2 from THA; A: SpO2 84% on 4L; R: I need you to evaluate now"). I-PASS is a *transfer-of-care tool* across a roster of patients. The distinction matters: SBAR optimises for one-shot escalation; I-PASS optimises for transferred responsibility. A residency that uses SBAR for sign-out is misusing the tool — SBAR's four elements don't carry illness-severity stratification or contingency planning, both of which the receiver needs across twelve hours. Knowing which form to deploy when is part of communication maturity.

### 9. The intervention is the *change in the artifact*, not training added to existing practice

The original implementation explicitly *replaced* whatever the residency had been doing. It did not layer I-PASS on top of unstructured sign-out as "additional training." This matters because most patient-safety interventions add work; I-PASS substitutes work. The receiver does not have to memorise five new things — they have to learn five things and stop doing whatever they were doing before. The trial's effect size depended on this substitution; programs that have added I-PASS as supplementary training without removing the prior practice have reported smaller effects.

### 10. The bundle is deliberately low-tech

I-PASS runs on a printed card and a piece of paper. There is no I-PASS app, no EHR module marketed as "I-PASS-compliant," no required new software. The deliberate low-tech-ness is a portability decision: a residency in a low-resource setting can implement I-PASS for the cost of printing cards. The choice also resists capture by EHR vendors — when handoff lives inside Epic or Cerner as a vendor-controlled template, the institution loses the ability to iterate the form, and the receiver loses the ability to use it offline (in a stairwell, at the bedside, on the phone). The low-tech default is a small piece of the bundle's durability.

### 11. The mnemonic survives translation

I-PASS has been deployed in Spanish, Portuguese, Mandarin, Arabic, and a dozen other languages — the mnemonic re-spells in each language while preserving the five-element schema. The Spanish version (Severidad / Resumen / Acciones / Atención a contingencias / Síntesis) loses the I-PASS acronym entirely but preserves the discipline. This is a deliberate design call: the *schema* is the intervention, not the spelling. A mnemonic whose mnemonic-ness depended on the English-language acronym would not survive the field's global adoption.

### 12. The artifact's authors made it free

The I-PASS Institute charges for training, certification, and implementation consulting; the artifact itself — the mnemonic, the sign-out template, the read-back script — is published in the NEJM paper and on the Institute's website without paywall. The decision to keep the artifact public is part of why the bundle spread to >50 hospitals across the four years following publication. Compare with proprietary handoff systems (some EHR vendors have attempted to capture this space) that did not spread. The artifact's openness is itself an apex move: the form survives because anyone can use it without permission.

## What it teaches

**For high-stakes communication artifacts (maps to wow-taxonomy 7.1 commit-before-reveal, 7.3 faded scaffold).** The receiver's synthesis (the closing S) is a *forced cognitive commitment* — the receiver cannot just nod; they have to produce. Read-back is the patient-safety analogue of commit-before-reveal: the receiver commits to their understanding, and the sender's listening then surfaces the gap. Borrow this pattern any time a transfer-of-responsibility artifact needs to verify the receiver's encoding rather than the sender's transmission.

**For mnemonic-driven artifacts in any domain (maps to taxonomy 2.12 invented notation with legend).** I-PASS is invented notation: five letters, five slots, exhaustive coverage of the receiver's needs, refused-by-design extensions. The discipline of *not adding a sixth element* is what makes the notation survive across specialties. When inventing notation, the test is portability — would your scheme survive the user who isn't your target user? — not local optimality.

**For artifacts that pair verbal + written + verbal-feedback (maps to playbook `clinical-handoff.md`).** The tri-modal architecture — sender speaks, paper holds, receiver speaks back — is the form's signature. Any high-stakes operational artifact (cockpit hand-off, nuclear-plant shift change, emergency-services dispatch) benefits from this triple encoding. A single modality (verbal-only or written-only) loses the cross-channel error correction.

**For evidence-based design discipline.** The 30% preventable-AE reduction is the artifact's argument for itself. Designers who want their artifact taken seriously by high-stakes communities (clinicians, pilots, plant operators) need to be able to point to a trial, not just to expert opinion. Build the form, then measure it; the measurement is part of the artifact.

**For the discipline of refusing additions (maps to taxonomy 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0 — at the schema level).** Every implementation pressures the form to add elements. The five-element refusal is what kept I-PASS portable. Subtractive discipline at the schema level is the cousin of data-ink discipline at the chart level: the form does its job *because* it refuses the extensions it could accept.

## What we'd steal

**The forcing-function ordering for transfer-of-responsibility artifacts.** When designing any handoff template — engineering on-call hand-off, customer-service ticket transfer, manufacturing-shift hand-off — start with severity, end with receiver synthesis. The middle elements vary; the bookends are the discipline. See `templates/react-handoff-ipass.tsx` for the production-ready implementation in our system.

**IF/THEN contingency pairs as the structured anticipation format.** Borrow this format any time an artifact needs to pre-authorise the receiver's response to anticipated events. The IF clause is the trigger; the THEN clause is the pre-authorised action. The structure prevents the failure mode of "watch out for sepsis" — which the receiver interprets differently from the sender — by replacing it with "if SBP drops below 90 for more than 10 minutes, give 500 mL LR and call the senior." See `templates/react-cross-cover-triage.tsx` for IF/THEN rendering at sideline-card density.

**The three-state classification as the alternative to free-text severity.** Stable / watcher / unstable. Three states, mutually exclusive, exhaustive. Borrow this pattern any time an artifact needs to constrain a sender into a comparable state-label. In our system, the badge styling at the top of every I-PASS patient block enforces this; the colored badge is the visual residue of the classification refusal.

**The read-back script as the closing element of any handoff.** The receiver speaks back the synthesis; the sender listens for what is wrong. In a written or digital artifact, this becomes a *gate*: the receiver cannot mark the handoff complete until they have entered a synthesis paragraph in their own words. Our `react-handoff-ipass.tsx` implements this as a literal read-back gate before the patient block can be marked received.

**The cross-modality coupling pattern.** Pair verbal exchange + paper artifact + verbal feedback. The pattern is portable to any high-stakes domain. Our system's handoff templates assume the digital artifact is *the paper* — the written backup — and that the verbal exchange and read-back happen around it. Designers should resist the EHR-vendor temptation to make the digital artifact *also* the verbal exchange; the modalities do different cognitive work.

## What we wouldn't copy

**The pediatrics origin doesn't generalise unchanged.** I-PASS was developed and trialed in pediatric inpatient settings. The illness-severity classification works for adult inpatient medicine, but the contingency vocabulary in adult ICU, oncology, or psychiatry can require different schemas. Borrow the form; rewrite the content. A pediatric watcher and an adult-ICU watcher are different cognitive objects.

**The five-element discipline can be wrong for very simple transitions.** A handoff of two stable patients between a residency intern and their senior at the end of a shift may not warrant the full bundle. The form's strength is in roster-scale handoff (more than the receiver can hold in working memory); for two-patient handoffs the overhead can exceed the benefit. Use SBAR-style escalation tools for small handoffs; reserve I-PASS for the rosters it was designed for.

**The bundle's success at Boston Children's was partly cultural.** The original implementation included intense faculty-development effort, simulation training, direct observation, and feedback. Programs that have adopted only the mnemonic without the cultural change have reported smaller effects (one meta-analysis: Davis et al., *Acad Med* 2017, found effect sizes 30-60% of the original trial). The artifact is not the whole intervention; the implementation work is half of what makes the form do its job. Don't borrow the form expecting the effect size without borrowing the implementation discipline.

## Cross-references

- **Playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-handoff.md` (Wave 11; the playbook this tear-down anchors)
- **Sibling playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/cross-cluster/operating-manual.md` (handoffs share the operating manual's read-back discipline)
- **Templates:** `templates/react-handoff-ipass.tsx` (4-patient evening sign-out with reducer-based I-PASS state, read-back gate, localStorage, PHI-redact toggle, markdown export); `templates/react-cross-cover-triage.tsx` (mobile-first 2 a.m. triage view with IF/THEN contingency rendering)
- **Library:** `references/libraries/medical-artifacts.md` §15 (Sign-out, cross-cover, and handoff artifacts); §12 (Inpatient note types)
- **Wow-taxonomy moves:** 7.1 commit-before-reveal (read-back); 2.12 invented notation with legend (mnemonic-as-notation); 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0 (schema-level subtractive discipline); A.4 clinical-personal dialectic (sender/receiver coupling)
- **Adjacent tear-downs:** `#36-mazur-peer-instruction.md` (commit-before-reveal in education); `#16-govuk-self-assessment.md` (one-question-per-page as forcing function); `#19-pathoma-condensed-review.md` (mnemonic-integrated content)

<!-- BEGIN: references/tear-downs/nih-r01-specific-aims.md -->

# Tear-Down: The NIH R01 Specific Aims Page

**Form:** the one-page Specific Aims document required as the leading page of every NIH R01 application; the document each reviewer reads in full before any other section
**Page-limit convention:** 1 page, single-spaced, 11-point Arial / Helvetica / Georgia / Palatino, 0.5-inch margins (NIH SF424 G.230 formatting rules; enforced by NIH eRA Commons at submission)
**Where to study:** the NIH OER "Sample Applications" archive (grants.nih.gov/grants/how-to-apply-application-guide/format-and-write/develop-your-budget/sample-applications.htm); the NIAID Sample Applications page; institutional grant offices' anonymised exemplar libraries; Hall et al., *Trends Microbiol* 2017 ("How to write a Specific Aims page that wins funding")
**Audience:** three readers — the primary reviewer who scores the application 1-9, the secondary reviewer who argues for or against the primary's score, and the >20 other study-section members who hear the discussion and vote without having read past the Aims page

## What it is

The Specific Aims page is a single-sided one-page document, structured as roughly twelve paragraphs, that opens an NIH R01 application. It tells the reviewer what scientific problem the lab is solving, why it matters, what the central hypothesis is, how three or four specific aims will test it, and what the world will look like if the work succeeds. Every R01 begins with this page; reviewers read it first, in full, under fatigue; the rest of the 12-page Research Strategy is read in depth only by the two assigned reviewers and skimmed by the rest of the panel from notes on the Aims page. The Aims page is the artifact that *the field reads*; the rest of the application is the receipts.

## Why it's apex

The Specific Aims page is the highest-density science-communication artifact in the federal funding system, and its conventions have hardened over fifty years into a form with the discipline of a sonnet. Apex Aims pages share four properties: (1) **the central hypothesis is one bolded sentence**, placed at the structural pivot of the page, that a reviewer can quote verbatim from memory the next morning; (2) **the twelve-paragraph anatomy is honoured** — problem, significance, gap, premise, hypothesis, approach, three aims, expected outcomes, impact, ROI close — and the order does work the reviewer relies on; (3) **the page is read in 90 seconds for the first pass**, and the structural compression assumes that timing; (4) **the writing is dense without being dense-feeling** — the reader's eye glides because every sentence is doing one job, not two. The form's authority is not in NIH guidance documents (NIH says only "one page"); it is in the accumulated craft of generations of PIs who learned the form by reading funded applications and by failing at unfunded ones.

## Decomposition

### 1. The twelve-paragraph anatomy is the form's spine

The standard anatomy, normalised across cancer, neuroscience, cardiology, infectious disease, and basic-mechanism R01s:

1. **Hook paragraph** — opens with the problem in the world (not in the literature). One to two sentences naming what is killing people, what is broken, what is unknown.
2. **Significance paragraph** — why the problem is large in scope and consequence; usually with a number (incidence, mortality, cost).
3. **Gap paragraph** — what specifically is not yet known; the gap *this application* will close. Names the gap precisely.
4. **Premise paragraph** — what the field *does* know that makes the proposed work feasible; preliminary data lives here in compressed form.
5. **Central hypothesis** — *one bolded sentence*; the structural pivot of the page. Everything before this sentence is setup; everything after is the test.
6. **Approach paragraph** — the overall experimental strategy; the model system; the methodological signature.
7-9. **Three aims** — Aim 1, Aim 2, Aim 3, each as one paragraph (occasionally a paragraph plus a one-sentence working hypothesis sub-bullet). Each aim is a coherent body of work, not a single experiment.
10. **Expected outcomes** — what specific knowledge the field will gain if the aims succeed; framed as additions to knowledge, not as papers.
11. **Impact paragraph** — what changes in the field, in clinical practice, or in public health when this knowledge is in hand.
12. **ROI close** — one or two sentences that reframe the investment: the small NIH cost against the large downstream value (new therapy, new diagnostic, new mechanistic framework).

A reviewer who has read 500 R01s decodes the page's argument from the *position* of paragraphs before reading their content. Paragraph 5 is the hypothesis because paragraph 5 is *always* the hypothesis. The form's stability is what makes 90-second reading possible.

### 2. The bolded central hypothesis convention

The single most identifiable convention in the form: the central hypothesis is set in **bold**, mid-page, often as one sentence. The convention emerged in the 1990s and is now near-universal in funded R01s. It serves three jobs: (a) it forces the PI to *have* a hypothesis (free-text prose can hide the absence of one); (b) it gives the reviewer an anchor for the second-pass read; (c) it is the sentence the secondary reviewer will quote at study section. Reviewers trained on the form expect bold; an Aims page without it reads as draft-quality even when the science is strong. The bolded sentence is also the test of the page's coherence — every aim that follows must be a test of the bolded hypothesis, not adjacent to it.

### 3. Innovation is *not* a paragraph on the Aims page

The Research Strategy that follows the Aims page has a required Innovation section. The Aims page does *not* — explicitly. Innovation on the Aims page is shown by the *premise* and the *gap* paragraphs: the gap names what is novel about what is unknown, the premise names what is novel about the methodological capacity. Reviewers who see an "Innovation" labeled paragraph on the Aims page read the writer as inexperienced; the convention is to weave the novelty into the gap and the approach paragraphs rather than declare it. The discipline of weaving rather than declaring is part of the form's compression — every paragraph does more than one job because the form does not have room for paragraphs that do only one.

### 4. The 90-second first read assumption

Study-section reviewers, after the introductory discussion of the application, have approximately 90 seconds before the primary reviewer's score is announced for the rest of the panel to form an opinion. They use that time to re-skim the Aims page. The page is structurally compressed for that 90-second skim: the bold hypothesis is locatable in 2-3 seconds; the three-aim structure is locatable in 5-10 seconds; the significance and impact paragraphs anchor the top and the bottom. A page that does not survive 90-second skimming is functionally invisible at study section, regardless of the quality of the underlying science. The form is calibrated to a specific reading environment that the writer cannot change.

### 5. Aim numbering is the cognitive scaffold

Each aim is numbered (Aim 1, Aim 2, Aim 3) and titled. The title is a *complete sentence*, not a phrase — "Determine the role of CYP3A4 polymorphisms in statin-induced muscle injury," not "CYP3A4 polymorphisms." The complete-sentence titling forces the writer to state what the aim *does*, not what it *concerns*. Reviewers cite aims by number in study-section discussion ("Aim 2's design is the weakness here"), so the numbering must be stable from the Aims page through the 12-page Research Strategy through the budget justification. An Aims page that loses aim numbering between sections breaks the reviewer's ability to refer to the work.

### 6. Three aims is the canonical count; four is risky, two is suspicious

Three aims is the modal R01 structure. Four aims signals an over-scoped application that reviewers will critique as unfundable in the proposed timeline ("the applicant has proposed a five-year program but described five years of work in two of the four aims alone"). Two aims signals an under-scoped application that reviewers will critique as not justifying the R01 mechanism. The three-aim convention is not in any NIH document — it is craft transmitted from PI to PI. There are exceptions (the methods-development aim plus two application aims; the two-mechanism-aim plus one clinical-translation aim) but the default is three.

### 7. Inter-aim independence is a defensive design

The strongest Aims pages structure the three aims so that *Aim 2 does not depend on Aim 1 succeeding, and Aim 3 does not depend on either*. Reviewers explicitly look for this: "What happens to Aim 2 if Aim 1's central finding doesn't replicate?" An aim structure where Aim 1's failure kills the whole application is a fatal weakness; an aim structure where the three aims independently test the hypothesis from different angles is a strength. The defensive design (aim independence) is also a substantive design — it forces the PI to triangulate the hypothesis with three different methods, three different model systems, or three different scales of resolution, which is good science as well as good grantsmanship.

### 8. The Expected Outcomes paragraph is *not* a results-prediction paragraph

Inexperienced writers turn the Expected Outcomes paragraph into a prediction of what each aim will find ("Aim 1 will show that ..."). Apex Aims pages instead frame Expected Outcomes as *what knowledge will be added* regardless of which direction the findings go ("Aim 1 will resolve whether the receptor is upregulated or downregulated under stress conditions; either result reframes the field's understanding of ..."). The framing matters because reviewers are trained to identify confirmation bias; predicting positive results across three aims signals a writer who has decided the answer before doing the experiments. The apex framing is *outcome-agnostic*: the knowledge gained is the deliverable, not the sign of the effect.

### 9. The ROI close is the underrated paragraph

The final one or two sentences of the Aims page should re-anchor the small NIH investment against the large potential downstream value. "Completion of these aims will identify the first pharmacogenomically-stratified intervention for statin-induced myopathy, affecting an estimated 5-10% of the 35 million Americans on statin therapy." The sentence is small enough to be quotable; the number is specific enough to be defensible; the framing converts the application from "more science" into "high-leverage science." Reviewers trained on the form notice when this sentence is missing or vague, and the absence costs the page on the Significance score. The ROI close is the page's only sentence that addresses the reviewer's question "why fund this rather than the equally good application in front of me."

### 10. Density without density-feeling

The Aims page must be dense — one page, single-spaced, ~750-900 words of substantive scientific content. But it must not *feel* dense. Apex Aims pages achieve this by sentence-level rhythm: the first sentence of each paragraph is short, declarative, scannable; the subsequent sentences elaborate. Paragraphs are 3-5 sentences; almost never 6+. The white space between paragraphs is the breathing room that lets the eye recover. The contrast with a poorly-written Aims page (one giant block of prose, sentence-stacked without breaks) is immediate. The form's discipline is *every sentence does one job*; pages that violate this become illegible even when the science is strong.

### 11. The page is calibrated to be read three times, not once

Reviewers read the Aims page three times: once when the application arrives (week 0), once when they begin the in-depth assignment (week 4-6), once again the morning of study section (week 10-12). Each read serves a different cognitive job: first-pass orientation, deep argument-building, recall-anchoring. The form's structure serves all three reads: the bolded hypothesis anchors recall, the three numbered aims structure the deep-read argument, the significance and impact paragraphs serve first-pass orientation. A page that wins on the first read but doesn't survive the third read fails at study section; the form's apex examples are designed for all three reads simultaneously.

### 12. The page is also the lab's strategic document

A subtle but important property: PIs who write apex Aims pages report using them as the *lab's strategic plan* for the funding period. The page becomes the document new postdocs read on day one to understand what the lab is doing and why; the document the senior grad student aligns their thesis chapters against; the document the PI re-reads at year three to assess progress. The Aims page is not only an application artifact — it is a lab-management artifact. The dual role is part of what makes the form take craft seriously: a sloppy Aims page produces a sloppy five-year lab plan.

## What it teaches

**For artifacts that anchor a long argument (maps to wow-taxonomy 1.1 editorial hero, 2.8 the reveal structure).** The Aims page is an extreme case of *opening as load-bearing structure*. The first paragraph names the problem; the bolded sentence names the hypothesis; the final paragraph names the payoff. The opening does the entire argumentative work; the body (Research Strategy) is the receipts. Borrow this discipline any time an artifact's leading page or section will be read while the rest will be skimmed.

**For documents whose conventions are craft-transmitted, not codified (maps to playbook `grant-application.md`).** Most of the Aims page conventions — the bolded hypothesis, the three-aim structure, the ROI close — are nowhere in NIH guidance. They are craft norms transmitted from PI to PI, study section to study section. Forms in any domain accumulate craft norms beyond their formal specifications; identifying which conventions are craft and which are rules is the first job of an applicant. The grant-application playbook in our system codifies what NIH does not.

**For density without density-feeling (maps to taxonomy 1.5 the quiet moment, 1.7 negative space as structure).** The Aims page's rhythm trick — short declarative first sentence per paragraph, generous paragraph breaks, white space as breathing room — is portable to any dense-but-scannable artifact. Our long-form documents (the tear-downs themselves, the playbooks, the library files) borrow this rhythm; the discipline transfers to product specs, RFCs, design documents.

**For outcome-agnostic framing (maps to taxonomy 2.10 the honest null).** The Expected Outcomes paragraph's discipline — framing what is learned regardless of effect direction — is the patient-safety analogue of the honest-null move in dataviz. The artifact gains credibility from *not* predicting positive results. Designers of any artifact that argues for an experiment, an intervention, or a change should borrow this framing: name what we learn if it fails, not just what we hope if it succeeds.

**For multi-pass reading calibration.** Designing for first-read + deep-read + recall-anchor reading simultaneously is a unique discipline. Most artifacts are designed for one read mode and fail at the others. Our reference files (playbooks, tear-downs) should be designed for all three: the section headings serve first-read orientation, the paragraph density serves deep-read argument, the cross-reference blocks at the end serve recall-anchor. The Aims page is the discipline's purest exemplar.

## What we'd steal

**The bolded central hypothesis as load-bearing structural element.** Any artifact whose argument hinges on a single claim should set that claim apart visually — bold, oversize, in a callout, in a different face — and place it at the structural pivot of the artifact. The discipline forces the writer to *have* a single claim, and gives the reader an anchor for re-reading. Our `templates/grant-specific-aims.md` enforces this with explicit bold formatting; the Aims page's pattern transfers to any thesis-bearing document.

**The 12-paragraph anatomy as a structured-prose template.** Reusable: hook → significance → gap → premise → claim → approach → 3 specific arguments → expected outcomes → impact → close. This skeleton works for any persuasive long-form document, not only grants. White papers, design proposals, project pitches, strategic memos can all use the structure. Our `grant-application.md` playbook captures the full anatomy with paragraph functions and word-count targets.

**Inter-aim independence as a design discipline.** When designing any multi-component artifact (three-section explainer, three-case argument, three-aim research plan), structure the components so that each stands on its own. The discipline forces deeper thought (each component must independently support the thesis) and produces a more defensible artifact (the whole doesn't fall on the weakness of any one part).

**The 90-second-first-read calibration.** Design dense artifacts assuming the first read will be skim-only. Make the visual hierarchy carry the argument; let the prose carry the receipts. The Aims page's bolded hypothesis + numbered aims + section-anchor paragraphs is one solution; our tear-downs use section headings, named decomposition items, and YAML blocks to serve the same skim-first calibration.

**The complete-sentence aim titles convention.** When labeling components of any structured document (aim titles, chapter titles, figure titles), use complete sentences that state what the component *does*, not phrases that state what it *concerns*. "Determine the role of X in Y" beats "X and Y." The discipline forces the writer to think about each component's contribution, and gives the reader an immediate understanding of each component's role.

## What we wouldn't copy

**The one-page rigidity is a constraint of the NIH submission system, not a discipline that generalises.** Many artifacts benefit from being *longer*, not shorter. Applying the Aims-page one-page constraint to artifacts that could legitimately be three or five pages truncates them artificially. The discipline of the form comes from optimising within the constraint, not from importing the constraint to artifacts that don't need it.

**The reviewer-fatigue calibration is specific to study-section dynamics.** The 90-second first-read assumption comes from a particular institutional structure (study sections with >20 reviewers, each carrying >10 applications). Most artifacts are not read under that compression. Borrowing the discipline (scannability, hierarchy) is portable; assuming the compression universally is not.

**The defensive aim-independence design can produce science-fitted-to-the-form pathology.** PIs sometimes structure aims for *application-defensibility* rather than for *scientific coherence*; the three aims that are most independent are sometimes not the three aims that best test the hypothesis. The form's pressure toward inter-aim independence is mostly good, but it can corrupt the underlying science when followed slavishly. Borrow the discipline only when it serves the artifact's substantive job.

## Cross-references

- **Playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/grant-application.md` (Wave 11; the playbook this tear-down anchors)
- **Sibling playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/long-form-document.md` (the Aims page's prose discipline is the long-form document discipline at one-page compression)
- **Template:** `templates/grant-specific-aims.md` (fictional R01 on statin-induced muscle injury; all 12 paragraphs with bolded central hypothesis)
- **Library:** `references/libraries/scientific-artifacts.md` (paper conventions; the Aims page's grandparent form is the abstract); `references/libraries/medical-artifacts.md` §21 (guidelines as designed artifacts)
- **Wow-taxonomy moves:** 1.1 editorial hero (the bolded hypothesis); 2.8 the reveal structure (claim up front, body earns it); 1.5 the quiet moment (white space as structural element); 2.10 the honest null (outcome-agnostic Expected Outcomes); 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0 (every sentence does one job)
- **Adjacent tear-downs:** `#04-distill-attention.md` (Distill's reference-grade typography is the academic-publication craft parallel); `#13-tufte-visual-display.md` (Tufte's self-publication is the academic-credibility parallel); `#36-mazur-peer-instruction.md` (apex artifact whose authority is borrowed from a randomized trial, as I-PASS is, as the Aims page is from the funded-grant dataset)

# Tear-Down: SCAT6 — Sport Concussion Assessment Tool, 6th Edition (Amsterdam 2023)

**Originating body:** Concussion in Sport Group (CISG), 6th International Consensus Conference on Concussion in Sport, Amsterdam, October 2022; published 2023
**Canonical citation:** Echemendia RJ, Brett BL, Broglio S, et al. "Introducing the Sport Concussion Assessment Tool 6 (SCAT6)." *Br J Sports Med.* 2023;57(11):619-621. doi:10.1136/bjsports-2023-106849 (with companion Child-SCAT6 for ages 8-12, and SCOAT6 — the Sport Concussion Office Assessment Tool — for the subacute office setting)
**Format:** single-page front-and-back paper card; >40 sites worldwide license-free reproduction
**Audience:** physicians, athletic trainers, physiotherapists, paramedics, and licensed healthcare professionals working in sport-related settings at all levels of competition from youth through professional
**Where to study:** the BJSM publication (open-access); the CISG consensus statement (Patricios JS, Schneider KJ, Dvorak J, et al. *BJSM* 2023;57(11):695-711); the Amsterdam 2023 conference proceedings; per-sport adaptations (FIFA, World Rugby, NFL/NCAA/NHL/NBA, AFL)

## What it is

SCAT6 is a single-page (front and back) standardised assessment tool for evaluating suspected sport-related concussion at the time of the suspected injury. It is designed for sideline deployment — no power required, no specialised equipment beyond a stopwatch and a printable card. The tool integrates eight components in a deliberate order: an immediate red-flag screen at the top, Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS), cervical spine assessment, Maddocks Questions (sport-specific orientation), the symptom evaluation (22 symptoms × 7-point Likert), the cognitive screen (immediate memory + concentration + delayed recall), the modified Balance Error Scoring System (mBESS), and the coordination/tandem-gait assessment. Each section flows into a clinical decision: any positive red flag stops the assessment and triggers emergency-services activation; the remaining components inform the *remove-from-play* decision; serial assessment over the subsequent hours and days informs the *return-to-play* progression. The tool is the global default for sideline concussion assessment and is recommended by FIFA, World Rugby, NCAA, NFL, NHL, and most national sports-medicine governing bodies.

## Why it's apex

SCAT6 is the apex example of a *constrained-real-world assessment artifact*: it must be deployable on the sideline of a high-school football game in Texas, a rugby pitch in Auckland, and an Olympic ice-hockey rink in Beijing, all with equal effectiveness. Four moves earn the apex rating: (1) **the red-flag screen at the top** is a *stop-trigger*, not a checklist — any positive flag (deteriorating mental status, focal neurological deficit, seizure, severe headache, neck pain with neurological signs) terminates the SCAT6 and activates emergency services, refusing the failure mode of completing the tool while a surgical emergency unfolds; (2) **the serial-assessment design** is explicit — SCAT6 is not a one-time test; it is repeated at multiple time points (immediate sideline, post-event, 24-48 hours, return-to-play progression) and the *trajectory* across assessments is the diagnostic signal, not the single-time-point score; (3) **the sport-specific Maddocks Questions** ("What venue are we at?", "Which half is it?", "Who scored last?", "What team did we play last week?", "Did we win the last game?") replace generic orientation questions because the sport-specific questions are *more discriminating* for the population — every athlete knows their venue and last opponent without injury, and forgetting them is a sensitive concussion signal; (4) **the deliberate-cognitive-load discipline** keeps the tool deployable under sideline pressure — the immediate memory test is 5 or 10 words (not a full neuropsychological battery), the mBESS is 60 seconds in 3 stances (not a full posturography assessment), and the whole tool deploys in 10-15 minutes (not the 60+ minutes a full neurocognitive assessment would require).

## Decomposition

### 1. The red-flag screen at the top is a stop-trigger

The first section of SCAT6 is a list of red flags: "Neck pain or tenderness," "Double vision," "Weakness or tingling/burning in arms or legs," "Severe or increasing headache," "Seizure or convulsion," "Loss of consciousness," "Deteriorating conscious state," "Vomiting," "Increasingly restless, agitated or combative," "GCS < 15," "Visible deformity of the skull." Any positive red flag *terminates* the SCAT6 and triggers emergency-services activation. The placement is deliberate: the red flags are the *first thing the assessor reads* because the SCAT6 is not what the patient needs if a red flag is present. The discipline matches aviation cockpit emergency procedures (engine fire on takeoff: the procedure is reject takeoff, not run the diagnostic checklist) and the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's pre-induction screen (Wave 13 batch, WHO Checklist tear-down). The stop-trigger discipline is portable to any assessment tool that operates in a domain where emergencies and routine findings coexist.

### 2. GCS as the anchor, not the metric

The Glasgow Coma Scale is recorded but is not the basis for the concussion diagnosis. Most concussion patients have GCS 15 (the maximum); the GCS is documented to *exclude* the moderate-to-severe traumatic brain injury that would warrant a different workflow (CT, observation, neurosurgical evaluation). The discipline distinguishes the *anchor* (GCS) from the *diagnostic instrument* (the full SCAT6). The anchor's role is to rule out the more severe diagnosis; the diagnostic instrument identifies the lesser one. The pattern is portable: many assessment artifacts benefit from an anchor metric that rules out the worst-case diagnosis before the artifact's specific assessment begins.

### 3. The Maddocks Questions are sport-specific

Generic orientation questions ("What's your name? What day is it? Where are you?") are too easy — most concussed athletes pass them even with significant cognitive disruption. The Maddocks Questions are sport-specific:

- "At what venue are we at today?"
- "Which half is it now?"
- "Who scored last in this game?"
- "What team did you play last week / game?"
- "Did your team win the last game?"

The questions are calibrated to information that an uninjured athlete knows automatically. Failure to answer these correctly is a more sensitive concussion signal than failure on generic orientation. The Maddocks Questions were validated in 1995 (Maddocks DL, Dicker GD, Saling MM. *Clin J Sport Med* 1995;5(1):32-35) and have remained the SCAT's orientation test across five revisions. The discipline is the same as Hoppenfeld's per-region exam (Wave 13 batch, Hoppenfeld tear-down): tailor the assessment to the population to maximise discrimination.

### 4. The 22-symptom × 7-point scale composite

The symptom evaluation lists 22 symptoms across cognitive, somatic, sleep, and emotional domains. Each symptom is rated on a 7-point Likert scale (0 = none, 6 = severe). The athlete (when able) rates each symptom; the total severity score (sum of all 22 × max 6 = 132) and the number of endorsed symptoms (max 22) are both calculated. The dual scoring (severity total + endorsement count) is the design move: an athlete with three severe symptoms and an athlete with twenty mild symptoms can have similar severity totals but different endorsement counts, and the trajectory of both across serial assessments carries different clinical information. The 22-symptom list is invariant across SCAT versions since SCAT3, allowing cross-study comparison.

### 5. The cognitive screen as three brief subtests

The cognitive screen is intentionally brief: immediate memory (5 words read 3 times, recalled after each reading; or 10 words for higher-level testing), concentration (digit spans backward; months of the year in reverse), delayed recall (the same word list after a 5-10 minute interval during which other SCAT components are completed). The choice of 5 vs 10 words is calibrated to the athlete's baseline; the digit-span and reverse-months tasks are standardised across populations; the delayed recall uses the time naturally elapsed during the rest of the SCAT, not a separate waiting period. The discipline of *fitting cognitive testing into the natural flow of the assessment* is what keeps the tool sideline-deployable; a separate 10-minute waiting period would break the workflow.

### 6. The mBESS for balance assessment

The modified Balance Error Scoring System (mBESS) is a 60-second balance test in three stances (double-leg, single-leg, tandem) on a firm surface; the assessor counts errors (eyes open within 20 seconds, hands off iliac crests, step/stumble/fall, hip abduction > 30°, opening eyes). The mBESS is the modified version of the original BESS (which included foam-surface conditions); the modification trades sensitivity for sideline deployability. The 60-second-times-three-stances design is the cognitive-load calibration discipline — long enough to detect significant balance dysfunction, short enough to perform under sideline conditions. The trade is honest: the mBESS misses subtle balance deficits the full BESS would catch; the SCAT6 is explicit that abnormal sideline mBESS is a *concussion signal*, not a *definitive diagnosis*.

### 7. The single-page sideline-deployable format

SCAT6 fits on a single double-sided A4 sheet. No power, no computer, no specialised equipment beyond a stopwatch. The format is deliberate: sideline conditions in many sports include rain, mud, crowd noise, time pressure (the game is paused for the assessment), and limited equipment. A tool that requires a tablet, an internet connection, or a quiet room is not deployable in those conditions. The format constraint is the discipline; the discipline produces an artifact that is *actually used* rather than an aspirationally-better tool that would only be used in idealised conditions. The pattern parallels the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's wall-mounted paper format (Wave 13 batch, WHO Checklist tear-down).

### 8. The explicit remove-from-play decision

After completing SCAT6, the assessor makes an explicit *remove-from-play* decision. The CISG consensus and SCAT6 are unambiguous: "Athletes diagnosed with concussion should not return to play on the day of injury." The decision is binary (remove from play / return to play), based on the SCAT6 findings plus clinical judgment. The "remove from play" decision triggers the subsequent Graduated Return to Sport (GRTS) protocol, a stepwise progression that takes a minimum of 7 days (24 hours per stage minimum) to complete. The binary decision discipline is what makes SCAT6 actionable — the tool does not produce a "concussion likelihood score" that the assessor must interpret; it produces a remove-from-play recommendation the assessor either accepts or overrides. The pattern matches the WHO Checklist's binary Sign In / Sign Out completion (the procedure either is or is not safe to start).

### 9. The serial-assessment design is the diagnostic signal

A single SCAT6 score has limited diagnostic value because individual variation is large. The diagnostic signal is the *trajectory* across serial assessments: the athlete's baseline (ideally established at pre-season testing), the immediate sideline SCAT6, a 24-48 hour post-event SCAT6, and the SCAT6 at each Graduated Return to Sport stage. An athlete whose immediate symptom severity is 30 but who returns to baseline at 48 hours has a different clinical trajectory than an athlete whose symptom severity is 15 but who is unchanged at 48 hours. The serial-assessment design is the artifact's *temporal* dimension: the SCAT6 is not a tool, it is a series of tool-deployments across a recovery timeline. The discipline parallels the I-PASS handoff (Wave 13 batch, I-PASS tear-down): both artifacts depend on repeated deployment with consistent structure to produce their diagnostic / coordination signal.

### 10. The Child-SCAT6 and SCOAT6 companions

The CISG publishes three coordinated tools: SCAT6 (ages 13+, sideline), Child-SCAT6 (ages 8-12, sideline, age-appropriate language and tasks), and SCOAT6 (the office-based subacute assessment for the days-to-weeks post-injury). The three-tool family covers the assessment continuum: acute sideline (SCAT6 / Child-SCAT6), subacute office (SCOAT6), return-to-play decision (Graduated Return to Sport progression). Each tool inherits the SCAT6 structural conventions (red-flag screen, multi-component composite, serial assessment) and adapts the specific tasks to the context. The companion architecture parallels Hoppenfeld's *Physical Examination* + *Surgical Exposures* pair (Wave 13 batch, Hoppenfeld tear-down): the tool family is more than the sum.

### 11. The license-free reproduction and per-sport adaptation

The SCAT6 is published license-free; teams, governing bodies, and clinicians can reproduce it freely. The CISG also explicitly invites per-sport adaptations (FIFA's adaptation includes football-specific Maddocks-equivalent questions; World Rugby's adaptation includes additional cervical-spine assessment given rugby's tackle mechanism; NFL's adaptation includes the unique-to-football considerations of helmet integrity). The discipline of *preserving the structural backbone while inviting content adaptation* parallels the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's per-country adaptations (Wave 13 batch, WHO Checklist tear-down) and the I-PASS schema portability. The license-free + per-sport adaptation strategy is what makes SCAT6 the global default; proprietary or sport-restricted alternatives have not displaced it.

### 12. The evidence-and-consensus dual basis

SCAT6's design rests on two evidence bases. The *clinical-research* base is the published validation literature on each component (Maddocks 1995 for the orientation questions; Echemendia et al. for the symptom-evaluation psychometrics; Iverson et al. for the BESS validation). The *consensus* base is the every-4-years International Conference on Concussion in Sport, which synthesises the prior 4 years of research and produces the updated tool. The dual basis is the discipline: pure consensus without evidence is opinion; pure evidence without consensus is academic; the combination produces a clinically-usable tool with both research grounding and clinical endorsement. The discipline parallels the ACC/AHA guideline process (Wave 13 batch, ACC/AHA HFrEF tear-down) and the WHO guideline process. The combination is the form's authority.

## What it teaches

**For constrained-deployment assessment artifacts (maps to playbook `physical-exam-maneuver.md`).** The single-page, no-power, sideline-deployable format is portable to any assessment artifact that must work in non-ideal conditions: roadside trauma assessment, mass-casualty triage tools, austere-medicine field guides. The format constraint produces deployability; deployability produces actual-use. Our `templates/react-return-to-play.tsx` (Wave 11) implements the Graduated Return to Sport progression that SCAT6 feeds; the format discipline transfers.

**For stop-trigger assessment design (maps to wow-taxonomy 7.1 commit-before-reveal).** The red-flag screen at the top of the artifact is a cognitive forcing function: the assessor *cannot* proceed with the SCAT6 until they have screened for the worse diagnosis. The pattern is portable to any artifact whose primary risk is treating-a-lesser-condition-while-a-greater-condition-progresses: financial-risk assessments, security audits, software-deployment checklists.

**For serial-assessment design (maps to wow-taxonomy 3.10 reproducible randomness — the temporal-trajectory analogue).** The diagnostic signal is the trajectory across repeated deployments, not the single-time-point score. The discipline is portable to monitoring dashboards, longitudinal health metrics, software-performance baselines. A single observation is noise; the trajectory is signal.

**For sport-specific / domain-specific orientation questions.** Generic assessment items often have ceiling effects; domain-specific items are more discriminating. The Maddocks Questions are the apex example: replace "what year is it?" with "who scored last in this game?" and you have a more sensitive instrument for the same construct. Borrow this discipline anywhere an assessment must discriminate within a specific population.

**For evidence-and-consensus dual basis (maps to wow-taxonomy 2.4 provenance transparency).** The tool's authority comes from both the research literature and the international consensus. The combination is more durable than either alone. Borrow this discipline for any artifact whose authority depends on clinical adoption: research grounding gives the artifact rigor; expert consensus gives the artifact legitimacy.

## What we'd steal

**The red-flag stop-trigger pattern at the top of any assessment artifact.** When the assessment domain includes both routine and emergency findings, the artifact must open with the emergency screen. Our `templates/react-return-to-play.tsx` includes a symptom-recurrence drop-back; the stop-trigger discipline could be added at the top of future SCAT6-derived templates.

**The serial-assessment design with trajectory as signal.** When designing monitoring or assessment artifacts, plan for repeated deployment with consistent structure; the diagnostic value is in the trajectory. Our `templates/react-return-to-play.tsx` implements the 6-stage Graduated Return to Sport with 24-hour timers per stage; the trajectory across stages is the artifact's signal.

**The domain-specific orientation pattern.** When the assessment population shares specific knowledge (athletes know their venue; clinicians know their patients; engineers know their stack), use domain-specific questions rather than generic ones. The discrimination is better.

**The single-page sideline-deployable format constraint.** Constrain the artifact to the deployment environment; the constraint produces the discipline. Our `templates/react-cross-cover-triage.tsx` borrows this for the 2 a.m. mobile-first triage view; the SCAT6 is the parent pattern.

**The companion-tool family architecture (acute / subacute / progression).** SCAT6 + Child-SCAT6 + SCOAT6 + Graduated Return to Sport is a four-tool family covering the recovery continuum. Borrow this discipline when an assessment domain spans multiple time horizons: a single tool cannot cover all of them, but a coordinated family preserves the structural backbone while adapting to each context.

## What we wouldn't copy

**The 7-day minimum return-to-play timeline is calibrated to the typical concussion recovery, not to evidence of necessity.** The 24-hour-per-stage discipline has clinical wisdom but limited direct evidence; individual athletes vary widely in recovery trajectory. Borrowing the staged-progression discipline is good; assuming the 7-day floor is evidence-based is overclaim. Our `templates/react-return-to-play.tsx` implements the GRTS protocol faithfully; the implementation should be honest about the evidence basis.

**The CISG's exclusion of younger ages from SCAT6 reflects developmental cognitive science.** The SCAT6 is for ages 13+ because the cognitive assessment items have age-appropriate variants. Child-SCAT6 exists for ages 8-12, but there is no SCAT for ages <8. Borrowing the SCAT6 format for very young populations requires age-appropriate cognitive items; do not assume the SCAT6 transfers down-age unchanged.

**The reliance on athlete self-report has known limitations.** Athletes have documented incentives to under-report symptoms (to return to play). The SCAT6 includes structured assessment to complement self-report (mBESS, cognitive testing) but the symptom evaluation is self-report. Borrowing the artifact requires understanding the incentive structure of the population; in populations with under-reporting incentives, the structured-assessment components carry more weight than the self-report total.

## Cross-references

- **Playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/physical-exam-maneuver.md` (Wave 11; the physical-skills-assessment parent playbook)
- **Sibling playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-algorithm.md` (Wave 10; the SCAT6 terminates in algorithmic remove-from-play / return-to-play decisions)
- **Templates:** `templates/react-return-to-play.tsx` (Wave 11; Amsterdam 2023 6-stage Graduated Return to Sport state machine that SCAT6 feeds); `templates/scat6-interactive.tsx` (Wave 13 sibling — pending creation by parallel agent in this wave)
- **Library:** `references/libraries/medical-artifacts.md` §11 (physical-examination artifacts); §18 (sports-medicine and concussion artifacts)
- **Wow-taxonomy moves:** 7.1 commit-before-reveal (red-flag stop-trigger as cognitive forcing function); 2.4 provenance transparency (evidence-and-consensus dual basis); 3.10 reproducible randomness (the serial-assessment trajectory as the diagnostic signal — temporal analogue); 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0 (every component earns its place on the single page); B.1 signature progression (SCAT lineage from SCAT to SCAT6 across five revisions over two decades)
- **Adjacent tear-downs:** `who-surgical-safety-checklist.md` (this Wave 13 batch; shares the single-page constrained-deployment format and the stop-trigger pattern); `hoppenfeld-physical-examination.md` (this Wave 13 batch; shares the physical-exam-assessment discipline); `i-pass-handoff.md` (this Wave 13 batch; shares the serial-deployment-with-consistent-structure discipline); `acc-aha-hf-2022-algorithm.md` (this Wave 13 batch; shares the evidence-and-consensus dual basis)
