<!-- BEGIN: references/tear-downs/uptodate-algorithm-box.md -->

# Tear-Down: UpToDate Algorithm Box Convention (UpToDate.com)

**Publisher:** UpToDate, Inc. (Wolters Kluwer Health), founded 1992 by Burton "Bud" Rose, MD
**Format under examination:** the *algorithm box* — the standardised single-screen decision figure embedded in UpToDate clinical chapters; >7,000 algorithm figures across the platform's >12,000 clinical topics
**Where to study:** UpToDate.com (institutional subscription; trial access available); compare with DynaMed Plus, BMJ Best Practice, and the Merck Manual Professional Edition as adjacent clinical-reference platforms; the UpToDate editorial-style document (internal, not publicly available, but the conventions are visible from any algorithm in the platform)
**Audience:** practicing clinicians (internal medicine, family medicine, hospitalists, specialty clinicians, advanced-practice providers) using UpToDate as the point-of-care clinical-decision-support reference

## What it is

The UpToDate algorithm box is the platform's standardised decision-figure convention: a single-screen flowchart embedded in clinical narrative chapters, using a consistent shape vocabulary (diamond decisions, rectangle actions, oval termini), constrained to roughly 5-7 decision nodes per algorithm, with an editorial caption beneath each figure that names the evidence base and the last revision date. Each algorithm is referenced from the narrative chapter by inline links ("see algorithm 2") and is also independently accessible from a sidebar "Algorithms" panel. When an algorithm cannot fit on a single screen, it decomposes via off-page connectors into named sub-algorithms; cross-references to *related algorithms* in adjacent chapters are linked. The whole convention is enforced by UpToDate's editorial process — algorithms are drafted by clinical authors, illustrated by an in-house design team, peer-reviewed, and updated on a continuous cycle alongside the narrative chapters they accompany. The result is a corpus of >7,000 algorithm figures with notable visual coherence and consistent navigability — a body-of-work scale that no academic-publisher equivalent has matched.

## Why it's apex

The UpToDate algorithm box is the apex example of *algorithm-as-corpus discipline*: not the one-off brilliantly-designed flowchart, but the editorial convention applied across thousands of figures to produce *predictable navigation* in a platform clinicians use under time pressure. Four moves earn the apex rating: (1) **the consistent shape vocabulary** (diamond = decision, rectangle = action, oval = terminus, with parallelogram for inputs) is invariant across the entire corpus, so a clinician who has seen one UpToDate algorithm decodes any other algorithm without re-orienting; (2) **the single-screen-or-off-page-connector rule** prevents the failure mode of the unscrollable mega-algorithm that overwhelms the user — when an algorithm grows beyond 5-7 nodes, it decomposes into named sub-algorithms that each fit on one screen; (3) **the editorial caption with evidence base and last-update date** beneath each algorithm makes the figure self-provenant — the reader sees not just the recommendation but its grounding and freshness without leaving the figure; (4) **the integration with the narrative chapter** — algorithms are referenced by inline link from the relevant chapter sections, are also independently navigable from the sidebar, and cross-link to related algorithms in adjacent chapters — produces a *navigable graph* of clinical decisions rather than a collection of isolated figures.

## Decomposition

### 1. The consistent shape vocabulary across the corpus

UpToDate algorithms use the ANSI 1970 / ISO 5807 flowcharting shape vocabulary, adapted for clinical use:

- **Oval** — start / terminus (the entry condition that triggers the algorithm; the final disposition the algorithm reaches)
- **Diamond** — decision (a yes/no clinical question)
- **Rectangle** — action (a recommended clinical action: order this test, give this drug, refer to this specialist)
- **Parallelogram** — input/output (lab values, vital signs, imaging findings — data the clinician brings to the algorithm)
- **Pentagon (off-page connector)** — link to a sub-algorithm (this branch continues in algorithm X)

The shape vocabulary is invariant. A clinician who has decoded one UpToDate algorithm decodes any other algorithm without legend lookup; the cognitive load of the *form* approaches zero, leaving the cognitive load for the *content*. The discipline parallels Netter's vascular-color convention (tear-down #17): the formal convention carries the navigation, and the content carries the substance. The pattern is portable to any technical-documentation corpus where the user encounters many similar artifacts.

### 2. The single-screen-or-off-page-connector rule

The constraint is structural: an algorithm that does not fit on a single screen must decompose via off-page connectors into named sub-algorithms. The threshold is roughly 5-7 decision nodes per algorithm; algorithms with more nodes are routinely split. The discipline matches cognitive-load research on decision-tree navigation: users perform worse on flowcharts beyond ~7 nodes (Miller's "seven plus or minus two" magical number, applied to visual decision space). The discipline costs *coherence* — the full clinical-decision space is fragmented across multiple algorithms — and pays in *navigability* — each individual algorithm is comprehensible at a glance. The trade is correct for the use case (point-of-care reference under time pressure); for a deeper-study artifact (a textbook chapter, a guideline document) the trade would be different. The discipline is condition-specific to the deployment context.

### 3. The editorial caption with evidence base and last-update date

Beneath each algorithm, the caption names: (a) the algorithm's title (specific, descriptive — "Initial evaluation of suspected community-acquired pneumonia in adults"); (b) the chapter the algorithm belongs to; (c) the evidence base (the guideline, the systematic review, the consensus statement the algorithm distills); (d) the last-update date (when the algorithm was last reviewed and revised). The caption serves three jobs: it positions the algorithm in the platform's information architecture, it grounds the algorithm in the evidence base (so the user knows *why* the algorithm makes its recommendations), and it dates the algorithm (so the user knows *when* the recommendations were last reviewed). The last-update discipline matters because clinical-decision-support platforms drift in evidence currency; the visible last-update date is the audit trail of the platform's maintenance.

### 4. The integration with the narrative chapter

UpToDate algorithms are not stand-alone figures. They are referenced from the narrative chapter at the relevant section ("For the initial evaluation of suspected community-acquired pneumonia, see algorithm 1") and are linked from a sidebar "Algorithms" panel that shows all algorithms in the current chapter. The dual access pattern matches actual clinical workflow: a clinician reading the chapter encounters the algorithm at the relevant decision point; a clinician returning to the chapter for the algorithm specifically navigates from the sidebar. The integration discipline distinguishes UpToDate algorithms from stand-alone flowchart collections (which are not contextualised) and from prose-only chapters (which lack the algorithm's actionable distillation). The pattern is the *algorithm-in-its-context* discipline.

### 5. The cross-references to related algorithms

Each algorithm includes inline links to *related algorithms* in adjacent chapters. The CAP-initial-evaluation algorithm cross-links to the CAP-treatment algorithm, the CAP-admission-decision algorithm, the sepsis-recognition algorithm (when CAP is severe), the antibiotic-stewardship algorithm, the CURB-65 / PSI risk-stratification calculator. The cross-reference structure converts the algorithm corpus from a collection of >7,000 stand-alone figures into a *navigable graph* of clinical decisions. A clinician landing on one algorithm can navigate to all related decisions without leaving the algorithm view. The graph structure is what makes the corpus more than the sum of its individual figures.

### 6. The deliberate 5-7 node constraint

The constraint is not in UpToDate's published editorial guidelines (I have not seen the internal style document), but is visible across the corpus: algorithms with more than ~7 decision nodes are routinely split. The constraint matches cognitive-load research, but it also matches the deployment environment — a clinician scanning an algorithm on a hospital workstation under interruption pressure has limited capacity for branching navigation. The 5-7 node algorithm can be *held in working memory*; the 15-node algorithm cannot. The discipline costs the platform some artificial fragmentation but pays in deployment reliability. The pattern parallels the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's 19-item cognitive-load calibration (Wave 13 batch, WHO Checklist tear-down) and the SCAT6's single-page constraint (Wave 13 batch, SCAT6 tear-down).

### 7. The printable PDF version of each algorithm

Each algorithm has a printable PDF version, accessible via a print icon. The printable version preserves the algorithm's visual layout, the shape vocabulary, the captions, and the cross-references (as page numbers in a print form, as URL footnotes in the PDF). The discipline matches clinician workflow: some clinicians prefer the printed algorithm for bedside reference, the conference-room teaching session, or the patient-handout discussion. The platform serves both digital-native and print-preferring users with one canonical artifact. The two-format strategy parallels AO Surgery Reference's web-and-print existence (Wave 13 batch, AO tear-down) and Stripe Press's web-to-print continuity (tear-down #25).

### 8. The editorial process behind the visual coherence

The visual coherence across 7,000 algorithms is not authorial; it is editorial. UpToDate maintains an in-house design team that renders all algorithms (clinical authors draft the content; the design team produces the figures). The standardisation is enforced at the rendering stage, not requested at the drafting stage. The discipline matches AO Surgery Reference's Davos-studio model (Wave 13 batch, AO tear-down): the visual coherence is a property of the institution, not of any individual author. The model is portable to any large-scale documentation corpus: separate the *content authoring* from the *visual rendering*, and enforce visual standards at the rendering stage. The discipline costs institutional capacity; the discipline produces corpus coherence.

### 9. The continuous-update cycle

UpToDate algorithms are updated on a continuous cycle, alongside the narrative chapters they accompany. When a new guideline is published (e.g., the 2022 ACC/AHA HF Guideline — Wave 13 batch, ACC/AHA HFrEF tear-down — was incorporated into UpToDate's HF chapters within weeks of publication), the relevant algorithms are revised, the new last-update date is stamped, and the change is logged in the chapter's "What's New" section. The discipline matches the AO Surgery Reference's last-updated-date stamping (Wave 13 batch, AO tear-down) and stands in contrast to print-textbook updating (years between editions). The continuous-update cycle is part of UpToDate's commercial value proposition — clinicians pay for the platform partly because the content is fresh — and is the editorial discipline that maintains the freshness.

### 10. The contrast with adjacent platforms

DynaMed Plus, BMJ Best Practice, and the Merck Manual Professional Edition are the major adjacent clinical-reference platforms. Each has algorithm figures; none has UpToDate's corpus scale or visual coherence. DynaMed's algorithms are more variable in style (visible authorial drift across topics). BMJ Best Practice's algorithms are well-designed but fewer in number. The Merck Manual's algorithms are stylistically dated. UpToDate's apex position in the corpus-scale + visual-coherence dimension is the artifact's competitive moat. The platform's market dominance (UpToDate has ~2 million clinician subscribers worldwide as of 2024) is partly a consequence of the algorithm corpus quality.

### 11. The hidden-from-public editorial style document

UpToDate maintains an internal editorial-style document specifying the algorithm conventions (shape vocabulary, color usage, caption format, cross-reference standards). The document is not publicly available — the conventions are visible only by observation across the corpus. The closed editorial-style document is an UpToDate competitive choice; the apex discipline (conventions enforced across a corpus) is not dependent on the document being closed. Our system's equivalent is the publicly-documented playbooks in `references/medium-playbooks/` — the editorial-style document is open, and the discipline is enforced by lint tools rather than by an in-house design team. The two approaches both produce corpus coherence; the open approach is more portable.

### 12. The algorithm-as-clinical-decision-support boundary

UpToDate algorithms are *reference* artifacts — they distill recommendations from guidelines, systematic reviews, and consensus statements. They are explicitly not *clinical-decision-support* (CDS) artifacts in the technical sense — they do not generate alerts, do not integrate with EHR patient data, do not produce per-patient recommendations. The distinction matters because CDS in the technical sense is governed by the FDA (under software-as-medical-device regulations); UpToDate algorithms are protected by the practice-of-medicine exemption because they require clinician interpretation. The boundary is part of the platform's design: the algorithm offers the decision pathway; the clinician interprets and applies it. The discipline parallels the WHO Surgical Safety Checklist's design (Wave 13 batch, WHO Checklist tear-down): the artifact provides the structure; the team applies the judgment.

## What it teaches

**For documentation-corpus visual coherence (maps to wow-taxonomy B.1 signature progression, 1.13 identity-system as artifact).** The discipline of corpus-scale visual coherence enforced by editorial standards (not by single-author execution) is portable to any large-scale documentation project. Our `references/medium-playbooks/` collection is the equivalent; the discipline of *enforce standards at the rendering stage* generalises beyond medical clinical-reference.

**For algorithm-and-narrative integration (maps to playbook `clinical-algorithm.md` + `long-form-document.md`).** Algorithms embedded in narrative chapters, with bidirectional navigation (inline link from chapter; sidebar access to all algorithms in chapter), are more useful than stand-alone algorithm collections. Our `clinical-algorithm.md` playbook documents this pattern; the discipline transfers to any reference platform where decision artifacts coexist with prose explanation.

**For the cognitive-load-constrained algorithm (maps to wow-taxonomy 2.13 no chart at schema level, applied at node count).** The 5-7 node constraint is the algorithm-level analogue of the WHO Checklist's 19-item refusal and the SCAT6 single-page format. Each is a deliberate cognitive-load calibration. Borrow this discipline anywhere a decision artifact must deploy under time pressure: keep the per-artifact decision space within working memory; split when the artifact exceeds the threshold.

**For the editorial-caption discipline beneath each figure (maps to wow-taxonomy 2.4 provenance transparency, 1.6 editorial caption).** Every algorithm has a caption naming its title, evidence base, and last-update date. The discipline grounds the algorithm in its evidence and dates it for the audit trail. Borrow this pattern for any reference figure (in technical documentation, in scientific publications, in design specifications) — the caption is small but load-bearing for the artifact's credibility.

**For the printable-PDF parallel artifact (maps to wow-taxonomy 4.7 responsive down to the tiniest viewport, applied across media).** The two-format strategy (web + print) extends the artifact across the user's deployment contexts without forking the canonical version. The discipline is portable to any reference artifact whose users vary in device preference.

## What we'd steal

**The consistent shape vocabulary across a corpus.** Our `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-algorithm.md` and `templates/mermaid-clinical-algorithm.mmd` enforce ANSI/ISO 5807 shape vocabulary; the discipline came from this convention. The shape vocabulary is the corpus-coherence backbone.

**The single-screen-or-off-page-connector rule.** Our `clinical-algorithm.md` playbook requires this as a hard gate; the discipline came from UpToDate (and from broader cognitive-load research). The pattern transfers to any decision-tree artifact in any domain.

**The editorial caption with evidence base and last-update date.** Borrow the caption format for any reference figure in our system. The lint tools enforce the metadata; the discipline came from UpToDate's editorial convention.

**The cross-references to related artifacts.** When a corpus has many related artifacts, build the cross-reference graph. Our tear-downs cross-reference each other and the relevant playbooks; the discipline borrows from UpToDate's algorithm-graph navigation.

**The continuous-update cycle with visible last-update dates.** Our reference artifacts should carry last-reviewed dates; the discipline of versioning is portable beyond medical reference.

## What we wouldn't copy

**The closed editorial-style document is a corporate choice, not an apex one.** Our system's playbooks are open; the discipline is enforceable by open documentation plus lint tooling. The closed-document approach is UpToDate's commercial moat; for non-commercial systems the open approach produces more durable corpus coherence.

**The single-publisher monolith is fragile to platform decisions.** UpToDate is owned by Wolters Kluwer; the entire algorithm corpus is governed by a single corporate entity. If UpToDate's editorial choices drift away from clinical needs, no parallel platform replicates the corpus. The single-publisher dependence is a known risk in clinical-reference platforms. Our system's distributed authoring (multiple agents, multiple humans) is more resilient at the cost of stricter editorial enforcement.

**The 5-7 node constraint is a default, not a universal.** Some clinical decisions genuinely span 15-20 nodes (complex differential diagnosis algorithms; staged-treatment protocols with many branching points). UpToDate's discipline of splitting at 5-7 nodes produces fragmentation for these cases. Borrow the discipline for the typical case; tolerate larger algorithms when the clinical complexity warrants. Our `templates/clinical-pathway-stepwise.md` accommodates 5-stage pathways that would not fit UpToDate's 5-7 node constraint as a single algorithm; the stepwise-pathway form is the alternative when the discipline breaks.

- **Sibling playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-pathway-stepwise.md` (Wave 11; the stepwise-pathway alternative for clinical decisions that exceed algorithm-node thresholds)
- **Adjacent playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/cross-cluster/_spine.md` (the spine + leaves architecture that algorithm-corpus governance approximates)

- **Library:** `references/libraries/medical-artifacts.md` §21 (guidelines as designed artifacts); `references/libraries/visualization-grammar.md` (clinical algorithm and pathway forms sub-section)
- **Wow-taxonomy moves:** B.1 signature progression (visual coherence across 7,000 algorithms over decades); 1.13 identity-system as artifact (UpToDate's editorial-style document is the artifact behind the corpus); 2.4 provenance transparency (the caption's evidence-base + last-update date); 1.6 editorial caption (the caption beneath each figure); 2.13 no chart at schema level (the 5-7 node constraint as subtractive discipline)
- **Adjacent tear-downs:** `acc-aha-hf-2022-algorithm.md` (this Wave 13 batch; the guideline-source that UpToDate distills); `ao-surgery-reference.md` (this Wave 13 batch; the parallel apex corpus in surgery); `who-surgical-safety-checklist.md` (this Wave 13 batch; the cognitive-load-constrained sibling at the checklist scale); `#09-stripe-docs.md` (the technical-documentation parallel for apex corpus coherence)

<!-- BEGIN: references/tear-downs/who-surgical-safety-checklist.md -->

# Tear-Down: WHO Surgical Safety Checklist (2008)

**Originating team:** Atul Gawande and the WHO Safe Surgery Saves Lives initiative; the validation trial led by Alex B. Haynes (Brigham and Women's Hospital / Harvard School of Public Health), with eight pilot sites across high-income and low-income hospital systems
**Canonical citation:** Haynes AB, Weiser TG, Berry WR, et al. "A surgical safety checklist to reduce morbidity and mortality in a global population." *N Engl J Med.* 2009;360(5):491-499. doi:10.1056/NEJMsa0810119
**Where to study:** the NEJM paper (free at PubMed); the checklist itself, downloadable from WHO (`who.int/teams/integrated-health-services/patient-safety/research/safe-surgery`); Gawande's *The Checklist Manifesto* (Metropolitan Books, 2009) for the design narrative; per-country adaptations (NHS, Australia and New Zealand College of Anaesthetists, Joint Commission accreditations)
**Artifact format:** a single laminated double-sided A4 sheet, hung on the operating-room wall or placed at the anaesthetic monitor; 19 numbered items distributed across three time-anchored phases

## What it is

The WHO Surgical Safety Checklist is a 19-item checklist organised into three time-anchored phases that bracket an operation: **Sign In** (before induction of anaesthesia), **Time Out** (before skin incision), and **Sign Out** (before the patient leaves the operating room). Each phase has a defined trigger event and a designated reader who calls each item aloud while the surgical team confirms verbally. The checklist was developed in 2007-2008 by Gawande's team at the request of the WHO, validated in an eight-site international cohort published in *NEJM* 2009, and shown to reduce major complications by 36% (from 11.0% to 7.0%) and inpatient mortality by 47% (from 1.5% to 0.8%) when implemented correctly. The artifact is a piece of paper, 19 items long, with no special technology required, designed to be read aloud in approximately 90 seconds at each of three points in a case.

## Why it's apex

The WHO Surgical Safety Checklist is the patient-safety field's most rigorously validated procedural artifact, and the design discipline behind it is the field's clearest case-study in *boring as load-bearing*. Four moves earn the apex rating: (1) **the three time-anchored phases** map the checklist to the natural pause-points of an operation (no induction without Sign In; no incision without Time Out; no leaving the OR without Sign Out), making the artifact's deployment a property of the workflow rather than an additional task; (2) **the 19-item count** is calibrated to the cognitive load of a multi-task team mid-procedure — short enough to read in 90 seconds, long enough to cover the failure modes — and the discipline of *refusing the twentieth item* is what kept the form portable across the eight original sites and across the thousands of hospitals that have since adopted it; (3) **the read-aloud requirement** with a designated reader is a forcing function — silent compliance is not allowed; the team must verbally confirm each item, which surfaces gaps that silent ticking would hide; (4) **the deliberate boring voice** — flat declarative items, no narrative, no warnings, no rhetorical adornment — is the form of intellectual honesty appropriate to a tool whose purpose is to prevent a sterile-field break or a wrong-site procedure, not to inspire.

## Decomposition

### 1. The three time-anchored phases are inseparable from the workflow

Sign In happens *before induction of anaesthesia* — the patient is awake, the anaesthetist is at the head of the bed, the circulating nurse confirms identity and procedure. Time Out happens *before skin incision* — the patient is anaesthetised and prepped, the surgical team is gowned, the surgeon is at the table holding the scalpel. Sign Out happens *before the patient leaves the OR* — the procedure is complete, the dressings are on, the team is preparing to transfer the patient. Each phase has a defined trigger event (induction, incision, transfer) that makes deployment automatic rather than discretionary. The discipline matters because checklist programs without trigger anchors — checklists at the start of the shift, or "before each case" without a more specific moment — drift in deployment. The trigger is the anchor; the anchor is the artifact's deployment guarantee.

### 2. The 19-item count is the cognitive-load calibration

Nineteen items across three phases (7 + 7 + 5 in the original layout). The number is the discipline's most-studied parameter: human-factors research on surgical-team cognitive load suggests that >20 items per checklist degrades compliance and deployment time, while <15 items risks under-coverage of common failure modes. The 19-item count sits in the productive zone. Subsequent adaptations have pressured the form to add items (specialty-specific items in cardiac surgery, pediatric items, surgical-fire-risk items); the WHO discipline has been to *let local adaptation add specialty modules outside the 19-item core*, preserving the core's count as the portability backbone. The discipline parallels I-PASS's refusal of a sixth mnemonic element (Wave 13 batch, I-PASS tear-down).

### 3. The read-aloud requirement is the forcing function

Each item is *read aloud* by a designated reader, and each item is *verbally confirmed* by the responsible team member. The Sign In's "patient identity confirmed" is not "tick if confirmed"; it is "I confirm Maria Garcia, MRN 184-23-9871, for right knee arthroscopy." The verbal confirmation surfaces gaps silent compliance hides — the team member who tick-acknowledges without actually checking, the team member who assumes someone else has confirmed, the team member who is mentally rehearsing the next case. Verbal confirmation also creates a witness trail: every team member hears the confirmation, can challenge it, and has heard the same set of facts. The read-aloud discipline is the same closed-loop communication principle as I-PASS's read-back synthesis.

### 4. The pause-discipline: the surgical team STOPS

Time Out is not a checklist read in the background while preparation continues. The surgeon stops; the anaesthetist stops; the scrub nurse stops; the circulator reads; everyone listens. The pause is the move. A Time Out performed while the surgeon is positioning the C-arm and the scrub nurse is opening the back table is a Time Out in name only; the deployment evidence in the NEJM trial depended on the *team-wide pause*. The discipline costs 60-90 seconds per case; the discipline saves wrong-site and wrong-procedure events that no other safeguard reliably catches. Hospitals that have downgraded the pause to background-reading have produced the documented "checklist fatigue" outcome — the artifact deployed but the effect not realised.

### 5. The designated reader role is intentional

The WHO checklist designates the *circulating nurse* as the primary reader for Sign In and Time Out, with the *surgeon* often leading Sign Out. The choice is deliberate: the circulator is not scrubbed (so they can hold the checklist), is present throughout (so deployment is reliable), and is hierarchically distinct from the surgeon (so the reader is not the most senior person in the room, which makes challenge across hierarchy easier). The designated-reader discipline matters because checklists without a defined owner drift to "whoever notices we should do it" — a coordination failure mode well-documented in aviation cockpit research. The designated reader is the deployment guarantee at the role level, matching the trigger event as the deployment guarantee at the workflow level.

### 6. The deliberate boring voice

The checklist's items are flat declarative statements. "Has the patient confirmed his/her identity, site, procedure, and consent?" "Is the site marked?" "Is the anaesthesia machine and medication check complete?" There are no warnings ("WARNING: failure to mark the site has caused wrong-site surgery"), no rhetorical adornments, no narrative explanation. The voice is procedural, calm, and structurally identical across all 19 items. The discipline matters because surgical-team members read the checklist hundreds of times per year; any decorative or warning voice would degrade through repetition into invisibility (the "warning fatigue" failure mode). The boring voice survives the repetition. Gawande's *The Checklist Manifesto* explicitly cites the aviation cockpit-checklist voice as the model: pilots read takeoff checklists tens of thousands of times in a career, and the cockpit-checklist voice is the boring-by-design discipline that lets repetition not erode the form.

### 7. The visible deployment: posted, not handed-around

The original implementation studies tested two deployment formats: posters mounted on the OR wall (visible to the whole team throughout the case) versus laminated cards handed around as needed. The *visible* deployment outperformed the *handed-around* deployment by a substantial margin. The reason is workflow integration: a wall-mounted checklist is in the team's peripheral vision throughout the case, prompting Time Out at incision and Sign Out at dressing-application without requiring anyone to remember to "get the checklist." The handed-around format requires explicit retrieval, which fails under workload. The discipline is portable: any checklist that needs to deploy reliably should be visible at the workflow point where it deploys, not stored elsewhere requiring retrieval.

### 8. Per-language adaptation, schema-preserved

The WHO Checklist has been translated into >40 languages; per-country versions (NHS, Australia, India, Brazil) preserve the 19-item core and the three-phase structure while substituting locally-relevant items (the NHS version, for example, includes a "VTE prophylaxis" item that aligns with NHS National Institute for Health and Care Excellence guidance). The discipline of *preserving the schema across translations and adaptations* is the same discipline I-PASS uses for its mnemonic. The schema portability is what makes a globally-deployed safety artifact possible; a per-country re-design from scratch would lose the shared evidence base and the cross-site comparability.

### 9. The deliberate refusal of EHR integration

Multiple EHR vendors have attempted to capture the WHO Checklist as a digital module: items as checkboxes, completion as a timestamp, escalation as an alert. The WHO has resisted, and the patient-safety literature supports the resistance. The reason is that the artifact's effect depends on the *team's verbal confirmation*, not on a database write. A digital checklist that records compliance without enforcing the pause and the read-aloud reduces to documentation theatre — the artifact's effect (the team-wide cognitive pause and the verbal confirmation) is the load-bearing element, and EHR integration tends to displace it. The discipline parallels I-PASS's resistance to EHR-vendor capture: the form's authority survives because the artifact is portable and modifiable; EHR-vendor capture would convert it to a proprietary, opaque, vendor-controlled object.

### 10. The implementation lessons, named honestly

The 36% complication reduction was the *aggregate* effect across the eight original sites; per-site effects ranged from no improvement (Toronto, the highest-baseline-quality site) to >50% reduction (Manila, with a much higher baseline complication rate). The honest reporting of per-site variation is part of the artifact's credibility. Subsequent implementations at high-baseline-quality sites have produced smaller effects, and a 2014 *NEJM* paper (Urbach et al., on Ontario hospitals) reported no significant overall improvement, prompting a productive debate about implementation fidelity vs. baseline-quality ceiling effects. The literature's honesty about heterogeneity is what makes the artifact survive the publication of negative-finding studies — the form's authority rests on the trial *and* on the implementation literature that explicates when the form works and when it doesn't.

### 11. The artifact spreads because it's free

The WHO publishes the checklist freely; no license, no fee, no proprietary version. The free-publication choice — like I-PASS's, like AO Surgery Reference's — is the artifact's spread mechanism. Proprietary surgical-safety artifacts have existed (commercial vendor products marketed as "safer than the WHO checklist") and have not displaced the WHO version even with marketing budgets. The free tier is a portability move; the discipline is the same across all three of this Wave 13 batch's anchor artifacts.

### 12. The form has spawned successors but has not been replaced

Since 2008, the surgical-safety field has produced enhanced checklists for specific contexts (cardiac surgery, obstetric emergencies, pediatric surgery), surgical-safety bundles (the Comprehensive Unit-based Safety Program [CUSP], the Surgical Care Improvement Project [SCIP] measures), and integrated team-training programs (TeamSTEPPS). None has displaced the WHO 19-item checklist as the global default. The reason is form-stability: the WHO Checklist solves a coordination problem that has not fundamentally changed since 2008, and the artifact's design discipline (three phases, 19 items, read-aloud, pause) is portable across the variation in surgical practice. The lesson parallels Hoppenfeld's longevity (Wave 13 batch, Hoppenfeld tear-down): form-stable artifacts in form-stable domains can outlive multiple generations of comprehensive updates.

## What it teaches

**For procedural artifacts that deploy under workflow pressure (maps to playbook `cross-cluster/operating-manual.md`).** The trigger-event-anchored deployment, the designated-reader role, and the verbal-confirmation requirement are portable to any procedural artifact that must deploy reliably across many users and many situations. Aviation cockpit checklists, nuclear-plant shift-change procedures, ICU rounds protocols — all benefit from these moves. Our `operating-manual.md` playbook codifies the deployment discipline.

**For the deliberate boring voice (maps to wow-taxonomy 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0, applied to prose).** Procedural artifacts whose purpose is repetition-survivable compliance should refuse rhetorical adornment. The voice's flatness is what lets it survive the hundredth read. Borrow this discipline anywhere a reader will see the artifact many times — operational dashboards, alert messages, standard-operating-procedure documents.

**For the 19-item count as cognitive-load calibration (maps to wow-taxonomy 2.13 no chart — at the schema level).** The discipline of *not adding the twentieth item* is the schema-level cousin of the dataviz discipline of not adding the redundant chart. Both are subtractive moves; both depend on the form's authority resisting the local pressure to add. Our `clinical-pathway-stepwise.md` playbook applies this at the step-count level; the same discipline transfers to any list-based artifact.

**For the visible-deployment requirement (maps to wow-taxonomy 3.12 section-as-control).** The wall-mounted checklist in the surgeon's peripheral vision is the deployment-environment cousin of the section-as-control move: the artifact must be at the workflow point where it deploys, not stored elsewhere. The discipline transfers to operational dashboards (place them on the wall the operators are already looking at), to documentation (place it in the editor the developers are already in), to safety signs (place them at the hazard).

**For the schema-preservation across translations (maps to wow-taxonomy B.1 signature progression).** The discipline of preserving the 19-item / three-phase schema across >40 languages and per-country adaptations is what makes globally-deployed safety artifacts possible. The lesson generalises to any artifact deployed across cultural or linguistic boundaries: the schema is the portability backbone; localisation is the content.

## What we'd steal

**The trigger-event-anchored deployment pattern.** When designing any artifact whose deployment must be reliable, name the trigger event explicitly. "Before induction of anaesthesia" beats "before each case." Our `operating-manual.md` playbook requires named triggers; the discipline comes from this artifact.

**The designated-reader role.** Name the role that owns deployment. "The circulating nurse reads the Sign In" beats "someone reads the Sign In." For digital artifacts, the analogue is the designated owner (the on-call engineer who runs the runbook; the release manager who runs the deploy checklist).

**The verbal-confirmation closed-loop pattern.** Items are read aloud and verbally confirmed; silent ticking is not allowed. The pattern is portable to any artifact whose effect depends on team-shared cognitive state — incident-response runbooks, mission-control launch checklists, surgical handoffs.

**The deliberate boring voice for repetition-survivable artifacts.** Refuse rhetorical adornment in procedural documents. The discipline is the voice's flatness; the lesson is the artifact's longevity.

**The schema-preservation discipline.** When the artifact spreads across contexts (languages, specialties, regions, institutions), preserve the schema; vary the content. The discipline is what makes a globally-deployed safety artifact possible.

## What we wouldn't copy

**The 19-item count is calibrated to surgical-team workflow specifically.** The cognitive-load research that supports 19 items is grounded in surgical-team dynamics: a team of 4-6 people, a 60-90 second pause window, a high-stakes near-zero-error-tolerance domain. Other contexts have different load profiles. A software-deployment checklist may tolerate 30 items because the reader is one person at a terminal; a fire-drill checklist may need to be 5 items because the readers are panicking. Calibrate the count to the deployment context, not to the WHO number.

**The wall-mounted physical artifact assumes a fixed workflow location.** Surgical teams work in a fixed location (the OR). Mobile or distributed teams cannot use the wall-mount deployment; the visible-deployment requirement has to be re-solved (in software, the equivalent might be a slack-channel-pinned checklist; on a mobile team, the analogue is a phone-lock-screen reminder). The discipline of visibility transfers; the wall-mount form does not.

**The implementation evidence is not the same as a deployment guarantee.** The 36% complication reduction is the validation-trial effect at high-fidelity implementation. Hospitals that have adopted the checklist as paperwork compliance have seen smaller effects or none. Borrowing the form without borrowing the implementation discipline (the pause, the read-aloud, the visible posting, the designated reader) produces documentation theatre. The artifact's effect is in the deployment, not in the document. Don't promise the 36% reduction from adoption alone.

## Cross-references

- **Playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/cross-cluster/operating-manual.md` (the operating-manual form this artifact anchors)
- **Sibling playbook:** `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-pathway-stepwise.md` (Wave 11; the sibling discipline for sequential pathways)
- **Playbook reference:** `references/medium-playbooks/clinical-handoff.md` (Wave 11; shares the read-aloud / verbal-confirmation discipline with I-PASS)
- **Templates:** there is no direct WHO-checklist template in our system, because the checklist itself is downloadable from WHO; for adjacent forms see `templates/mermaid-clinical-algorithm.mmd` (Hour-1 sepsis bundle as algorithm) and `templates/react-clinical-algorithm.tsx` (HEART pathway interactive)
- **Library:** `references/libraries/medical-artifacts.md` §17 (perioperative artifacts); §20 (patient-safety artifacts)
- **Wow-taxonomy moves:** 2.7 data-ink ratio approaches 1.0 (every item earns its place); 2.13 no chart (the discipline of refusing the twentieth item); 3.12 section-as-control (the wall-mounted artifact as visible-deployment); B.1 signature progression (schema preservation across translations and adaptations); 7.1 commit-before-reveal (the team's verbal confirmation as cognitive commitment)
- **Adjacent tear-downs:** `#36-mazur-peer-instruction.md` (commit-before-reveal in education; the WHO checklist applies the same discipline to surgical safety); `i-pass-handoff.md` (this Wave 13 batch; shares the read-aloud / verbal-confirmation discipline and the schema-portability move); `ao-surgery-reference.md` (this Wave 13 batch; the surgical-procedure detail the checklist brackets)
